Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

They keep saying they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain, which is frustrating because in my head it’s connected. Like, texture and money are connected sometimes, especially with materials, but then I lose the thread and… yeah. That’s why I came in.

Yeah… I think so. It comes in these bursts where I’ll suddenly take on, like, three proposals at once, and then I’m reorganizing the whole studio because the shelves are “wrong,” and then I’m sketching all night.

Sometimes, yeah. Not like a big booming voice or anything. It’s more like when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll hear this woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves.

And the shelves are kind of a mess, so at first I think maybe someone came in, or it’s the building making noise, or—well, the studio has weird acoustics because of the concrete and all the wood stacked up. But it feels real enough that I go check, and there’s no one there.

Sometimes it’s not a voice exactly, it’s like I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but nothing is on. That really bothers me because I start worrying about safety, but then I check and there’s nothing.

Yeah… I do get that. Like there are stretches where I feel really lit up, almost like everything clicks and I’m unstoppable, and every idea I have feels brilliant—like I can see the whole piece finished before anyone else understands it.

But yeah, I guess that is more energized than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Not like a big booming voice or anything. It’s more like when I’m alone in the studio, I’ll hear this woman quietly commenting, like from behind the shelves.\n\nAnd the shelves are kind of a mess, so at first I think maybe someone came in, or it’s the building making noise, or—well, the studio has weird acoustics because of the concrete and all the wood stacked up. But it feels real enough that I go check, and there’s no one there.\n\nSometimes it’s not a voice exactly, it’s like I smell hot metal near an unfinished piece, like something’s been heated, but nothing is on. That really bothers me because I start worrying about safety, but then I check and there’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high, euphoric, or irritable lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get that. Like there are stretches where I feel really lit up, almost like everything clicks and I’m unstoppable, and every idea I have feels brilliant—like I can see the whole piece finished before anyone else understands it.\n\nBut then, weirdly, if a collaborator makes some small comment, like about changing a material or “maybe simplifying,” I can snap. Not always yelling, but I get sharper than I mean to, and afterward it feels bigger than what they actually said.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, during those times I can sleep, like… two or three hours after working late, and then I wake up weirdly bright, like I’m ready to just keep going.\n\nIt’s not like when I pull a normal all-nighter and feel gross and shaky. I’ll get up and start thinking about the piece again, or the layout, or whatever I left unfinished, and I don’t really feel tired the way I usually would.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard that woman’s voice at a time when your mood felt normal—not especially energized, high, or irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. There was this quieter stretch, like maybe a month, where I wasn’t all revved up like that. I was sleeping more normally, not feeling like I could do everything at once.\n\nBut I still heard her sometimes, those little comments from behind the shelves. And I kept getting… I don’t know, messages isn’t the right word, but I’d look at normal studio stuff—scraps, labels, marks on wood—and feel like there were hidden warnings in them. That went on for a few weeks, even though my mood felt pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: At any time, have the voice and hidden warnings continued for at least two weeks when you were not feeling especially high, energized, or irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think it did. And I’d still look at regular studio things—labels, scraps, marks in the wood—and feel like they were warning me about something. and there’s nothing there.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: I came in because my artist statements, client notes, and even what I say in meetings have started jumping from textures to budgets to secret meanings, and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: There are days I spend an hour moving the same tools into tiny rows, tracing the same square in the air, or pacing around an installation instead of actually building it, even though I know the deadline is slipping.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: For stretches I feel lit up and almost unstoppable, like every idea is brilliant, but then a small comment from a collaborator can make me snap in a way that feels bigger than the situation.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I’ll suddenly take on three proposals, reorganize the studio, sketch all night, email curators, and start cutting materials for pieces I haven’t even budgeted yet.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: At my most charged up, I truly feel like I’m about to change how everyone understands installation art and that normal limits like funding, time, or experience don’t really apply to me.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: I can sleep two or three hours after working late and wake up feeling bright and ready to keep going, not tired the way I normally would be.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My thoughts can move so fast that images, titles, grant language, colors, and conversations all stack on top of each other before I can finish one idea.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Looking back over this whole period, the energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have taken up more of the year than the calmer stretches, even when the intensity rises and falls.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a quieter month when I was sleeping more normally and not feeling unstoppable, but I still kept hearing comments from behind the shelves and reading hidden warnings into ordinary studio materials for several weeks.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have missed paid deadlines, confused collaborators in meetings, and had an exhibition postponed because my notes and proposals stopped making enough sense to use.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n and there’s nothing there.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: I came in because my artist statements, client notes, and even what I say in meetings have started jumping from textures to budgets to secret meanings, and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: There are days I spend an hour moving the same tools into tiny rows, tracing the same square in the air, or pacing around an installation instead of actually building it, even though I know the deadline is slipping.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: For stretches I feel lit up and almost unstoppable, like every idea is brilliant, but then a small comment from a collaborator can make me snap in a way that feels bigger than the situation.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I’ll suddenly take on three proposals, reorganize the studio, sketch all night, email curators, and start cutting materials for pieces I haven’t even budgeted yet.\n- key: S035\n  name: like I’m about to change how everyone understands installation art and that normal limits like funding, time, or experience don’t really apply to me.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: I can sleep two or three hours after working late and wake up feeling bright and ready to keep I was sleeping more normally, not feeling like I could do everything at once.\n\nBut I still heard her sometimes, those little comments from behind the shelves. And I kept getting… I don’t know, messages isn’t the right word, but I’d look at normal studio stuff—scraps, labels, marks on wood—and feel like there were hidden warnings in them. That went on for a few weeks, even though my mood felt pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: At any time, have the voice and hidden warnings continued for at least two weeks when you were not feeling especially high, energized, or irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think it did. There was that quieter month where I wasn’t in that “I can do anything” mode, and I was sleeping more normally, not staying up with all the ideas racing.\n\nBut the voice was still there sometimes, like little comments from behind the shelves. And I’d still look at regular studio things—labels, scraps, marks in the wood—and feel like they were warning me about something. It wasn’t the whole month every second, but it was going on for several weeks, definitely more than two.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day nearly every day… I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure, where everything felt kind of gray and I didn’t want to deal with people.\n\nBut two full weeks like that, most of the day? I’m not really sure. Time gets weird for me when I’m in the studio, because one bad afternoon can feel like three days, and then I’ll get distracted by a project or errands or, like, reorganizing supplies for no reason. It went on for several weeks, even when my mood felt mostly normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I space out, but I think everyone does that, especially when I’m working on something repetitive. I mean, I’ve bumped my head before, like normal stuff—cabinet doors, low shelves in the studio, that kind of thing—but not where I blacked out or woke up on the floor or anything.\n\nUnless you mean something really brief? I don’t remember losing consciousness.\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, do you find yourself much more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Yes… there was at least one month like that, I think.

It was quieter, not the whole “I can do ten things and never sleep” kind of period. I was sleeping more normally, and I didn’t feel unstoppable or especially wound up. And I’d still look at regular studio things—labels, scraps, marks in the wood—and feel like they were warning me about something. It wasn’t the whole month every second, but it was going on for several weeks, definitely more than two.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day nearly every day… I mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure, where everything felt kind of gray and I didn’t want to deal with people.\n\nBut two full weeks like that, most of the day? I’m not really sure. Time gets weird for me when I’m in the studio, because one bad afternoon can feel like three days, and then I’ll get distracted by a project or errands or, like, reorganizing supplies for no reason. So… I can’t say clearly yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the voice or the hidden warnings lasted while your mood felt normal for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what I mean about that quieter month. I wasn’t feeling, like, unstoppable or wired, and I was sleeping more normally.\n\nBut even then, the voice would still pop up sometimes—comments from behind the shelves—and I’d still look at ordinary studio stuff, like scraps or labels or marks in wood, and feel like there were warnings in them. I mean, I’ve had moments where I space out, but I think everyone does that, especially when I’m working on something repetitive. Like sanding or sorting colors, I can lose track of time.\n\nBut actual seizures, like what people mean by that—I haven’t noticed anything like that, or no one’s told me I had one.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any drugs like cannabis, amphetamines, cocaine, LSD, or other substances around the times these experiences happen?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really? I don’t think there’s a pattern like that.\n\nI’m not, like, doing cocaine or LSD or anything. Cannabis… I’ve been around it at parties, but parties are weird because everyone’s talking too loud and the music makes the walls feel busy, so I usually just end up in the kitchen looking at the magnets on the fridge. But I can’t say those experiences happen because of drugs.\n\nI don’t know if caffeine counts, because I drink too much coffee sometimes when I’m working, but that’s not really what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any head injury where you lost consciousness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. That went on for several weeks even when my mood wasn’t high or irritable.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period lasting at least one week when you felt unusually high or irritable most of the day, nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"For stretches I feel lit up and almost unstoppable, like every idea is brilliant, but then a small comment from a collaborator can make me snap in a way that feels bigger than the situation.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like every idea I have is suddenly the right one, like I can see the whole shape of a project at once and I’m almost unstoppable.

And then—sorry, this is a little embarrassing—but if someone in the studio says one small thing, like a collaborator questions a color choice or asks if I’m sure about a direction, I can snap. Like way bigger than the comment deserves. It’s not just annoyed, it’s like my whole body goes sharp.

I’m not sure I’d always track it as “one week” neatly, because time gets weird when I’m working, but yes, I’ve had periods like that where it’s most of the day, nearly every day for a while.

Time gets weird for me when I’m in the studio, because one bad afternoon can feel like three days, and then I’ll get distracted by a project or errands or, like, reorganizing supplies for no reason. So… I can’t say clearly yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when the voice or the hidden warnings lasted while your mood felt normal for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s what I mean about that quieter month. It went on for several weeks, even when my mood felt mostly normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I space out, but I think everyone does that, especially when I’m working on something repetitive. Like sanding or sorting colors, I can lose track of time.\n\nBut actual seizures, like what people mean by that—I haven’t noticed anything like that, or no one’s told me I had one.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any drugs like cannabis, amphetamines, cocaine, LSD, or other substances around the times these experiences happen?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really? I don’t think there’s a pattern like that.\n\nI’m not, like, doing cocaine or LSD or anything. But I can’t say those experiences happen because of drugs.\n\nI don’t know if caffeine counts, because I drink too much coffee sometimes when I’m working, but that’s not really what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any head injury where you lost consciousness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve bumped my head before, like normal stuff—cabinet doors, low shelves in the studio, that kind of thing—but not where I blacked out or woke up on the floor or anything.\n\nUnless you mean something really brief? I don’t remember losing consciousness.\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, do you find yourself much more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll find scraps from one project mixed with invoices and half-labeled paint tubes, and then I’ll decide I need a better system, and that becomes another whole thing. But I can’t say those experiences happen because of drugs.\n\nI don’t know if caffeine counts, because I drink too much coffee sometimes when I’m working, but that’s not really what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any head injury where you lost consciousness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I don’t remember losing consciousness.\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, do you find yourself much more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s like I suddenly have… a whole engine running.\n\nI’ll take on three proposals at once, and then I’m reorganizing the studio because the shelves “need” to make sense, and then I’m sketching all night. And while I’m doing that I’ll email curators, start cutting materials for pieces I haven’t even budgeted for yet—like, I know that sounds impractical, but in the moment it feels like I can finally see how everything should fit.\n\nThe studio gets kind of chaotic, actually. I’ll find scraps from one project mixed with invoices and half-labeled paint tubes, and then I’ll decide I need a better system, and that becomes another whole thing. So… yes, much more active than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the hidden warnings been happening altogether for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. More than a month, definitely.\n\nIt’s hard to pin down because I didn’t write it down at first—I was trying to pretend it was just the studio being weird, like the old pipes and the smell of solvents, and I kept blaming the materials. But the hidden warnings in the textures and the voice, or sometimes those smells in the studio… that’s been going on for, I guess, around eleven months now.\n\nAlmost a year, which sounds worse when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I’ve had stretches where I felt really down or kind of stuck, especially when work wasn’t going well or I’d get behind on applications—those deadlines all pile up and then the studio starts feeling like this accusing place, which sounds dramatic, but—\n\nSorry. Like way bigger than the comment deserves. They’ve been happening on and off for, I’d say, about ten months now.\n\nIt’s like I’ll have too little sleep and too many projects going, and I don’t realize how long it’s been until the apartment is covered in sketches and half-finished materials. I keep thinking, “I just need one more day,” and then suddenly it’s been more than a week.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of losing time or awareness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I lose track of time when I’m working, like I’ll start cleaning brushes and then suddenly the light in the room is different, but that feels more like getting absorbed, not like I blacked out.\n\n“Losing awareness” sounds more serious than what I’ve noticed. Unless you mean forgetting why I walked into a room, because I do that constantly, but I think everyone does, right? Especially if there are too many things on the floor or I’m trying to remember where I put the smaller palette knives. So… no, I haven’t noticed episodes like that, I don’t think.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the time since these experiences began, have you spent most of that time in a clearly high or irritable mood?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 2\nmanifestation: \"Looking back over this whole period, the energized, irritable, overproductive stretches have taken up more of the year than the calmer stretches, even when the intensity rises and falls.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think, looking back, more of the time has been like that than not. Not always at the same volume, if that makes sense—some weeks it’s really obvious, like I’m buzzing and everything irritates me, and other times it’s more just this push underneath everything.

But if I’m honest, the calmer stretches have been shorter. I kept telling myself I was just in a productive phase, or that it was because I had shows to prepare for—though honestly there’s always some reason, some deadline, some thing with the studio space—but across the whole period, I think I’ve spent more of it high-strung and overproductive than actually settled.

There are stretches where I feel really lit up, like every idea I have is suddenly the right one, like I can see the whole shape of a project at once and I’m almost unstoppable.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is a little embarrassing—but if someone in the studio says one small thing, like a collaborator questions a color choice or asks if I’m sure about a direction, I can snap. Like way bigger than the comment deserves. It’s not just annoyed, it’s like my whole body goes sharp.\n\nI’m not sure I’d always track it as “one week” neatly, because time gets weird when I’m working, but yes, I’ve had periods like that where it’s most of the day, nearly every day for a while.\n\nDoctor: During one of those energized periods, did that state last at least one full week?\n\nPatient: Yes… not every single one was perfectly measured, but those energized, irritable stretches have definitely lasted at least a week. They’ve been happening on and off for, I’d say, about ten months now.\n\nIt’s like I’ll have too little sleep and too many projects going, and I don’t realize how long it’s been until the apartment is covered in sketches and half-finished materials. I keep thinking, “I just need one more day,” and then suddenly it’s been more than a week.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of losing time or awareness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I lose track of time when I’m working, like I’ll start cleaning brushes and then suddenly the light in the room is different, but that feels more like getting absorbed, not like I blacked out.\n\n“Losing awareness” sounds more serious than what I’ve noticed. Unless you mean forgetting why I walked into a room, because I do that constantly, but I think everyone does, right? Especially if there are too many things on the floor or I’m trying to remember where I put the smaller palette knives. So… no, I haven’t noticed episodes like that, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Over the time since these experiences began, have you spent most of that time in a clearly high or irritable mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think, looking back, more of the time has been like that than not. Not always at the same volume, if that makes sense—some weeks it’s really obvious, like I’m buzzing and everything irritates me, and other times it’s more just this push underneath everything.\n\nBut if I’m honest, the calmer stretches have been shorter. I kept telling myself I was just in a productive phase, or that it was because I had shows to prepare for—though honestly there’s always some reason, some deadline, some thing with the studio space—but across the whole period, I think I’ve spent more of it high-strung and overproductive than actually settled."
